@@779187 <p> Barack Obama and Bill Clinton are n't exactly friends . The former Democratic president has sometimes campaigned on behalf of his current successor , but after Mr. Obama defeated Mr. Clinton 's wife , Hillary , for the Democratic presidential nomination in 2008 , the Big He ( as Mr. Clinton was nicknamed in his carousing days in the Oval Office ) , famously told friends that the former Illinois senator would have to " kiss my ass " if he wanted Mr. Clinton 's help in that year 's presidential contest . Even as recently as the 2010 Congressional elections , the two men backed different Democratic candidates in local and state elections , such as Colorado 's Senate race . <p> So it 's not clear to what extent Mr. Obama heeds any advice Mr. Clinton offers , but let 's hope he takes this recommendation : Speaking at a U.S. Department of Energy conference in Maryland on Wednesday , Mr. Clinton urged the Obama administration to " embrace " the Keystone XL pipeline while as a trade-off insisting on " a stakeholder-driven system of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ environmental concerns of Nebraskans . Mr. Clinton admitted to being puzzled at how the pipeline had become so bogged down in Washington politics ( a phony bewilderment if ever there was one since it is Mrs. Clinton 's State department where the project has become swamped ) . <p> Mr. Clinton blamed the project 's Canadian backers , TransCanada Corp . for the delays . ( No sense blaming his wife or her department or even her boss , Mr. Obama . ) " One of the most amazing things to me about this Keystone pipeline deal is that they ever filed that route in the first place since they could 've gone around the Nebraska Sand Hills and avoided most of the dangers , no matter how imagined , to the Ogallala aquifer with a different route , which I presume we 'll get now , because the extra cost of running is infinitesimal compared to the revenue that will be generated over a long period of time . " <p> As an aside , I find it very telling that Mr. Clinton describes the " dangers " posed to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " -- i.e. imaginary , not real . Still , the practical politician in him understands they need to be addressed anyway and that the cost of doing so is tiny when set against the potential profits over time . <p> But another phony aspect of Mr. Clinton 's remarks is his assertion that TransCanada erred by proposing a route through , instead of around , the Sand Hills eco area . First of all , as Mrs. Clinton 's State department admits , there are more than four dozen pipelines of one sort or another -- oil , gas , water , etc. -- already crisscrossing the Sand Hills or the giant underground river cited by environmentalists and other opponents of Keystone . And , second , State spent three years from 2008 to 2011 examining all the environmental , social and economic impacts of the Sand Hills route and six others that avoided the sensitive area . In its FEIS ( Final Environmental Impact Statement ) , released last August , State said there was no greater risk from the direct , Sand Hills path than from the other @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ According to State , " The analyses of potential impacts associated with construction and normal operation of the proposed Project suggest that there would be no significant impacts to most resources along the proposed Project corridor . " <p> Any suggestion to the contrary is pure politics . <p> But whether or not rerouting Keystone is necessary from an environmental or engineering standpoint , it is necessary from a political standpoint . And Mr. Clinton , being a master politician , sees that quite clearly . And like it or not , he 's probably right . <p> None of this will come as news to TransCanada , though . I suspect they already knew politics would be as important to their successful completion of Keystone as engineering concerns and the need to maximize their shareholders ' return . But if they did n't grasp that before presidential-election politics delayed approval of their $7-billion project , they understand it now . The company will , almost certainly , shortly file a new request for a permit to build KXL that will include a right-of-way around the Sand Hills . <p> What @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that it further backs Mr. Obama into a corner . Now the president stands almost alone against the pipeline with only radical environmentalists for support . Most Republicans , of course , are in favour of Keystone and a majority of Democrats back it and the jobs it will produce so long as it is redirected through less environmentally sensitive lands . Even the Nebraska ranchers and other residents who oppose the line can probably be convinced to accept it so long as it avoids the Sand Hills and the Ogallala . Only eco-activists who want desperately to stop Canada 's oilsands from being developed are left behind Mr. Obama because they , erroneously , see the ' sands as a make or break moment in their fight against global warming and climate change . <p> Mr. Obama may still be encouraged to delay the pipeline until after this fall 's presidential vote . He and his campaign strategists may calculate that their potential margin of victory is still so slim that they must have the radical greens on their side on election day if they are to return to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ other supporters who want the pipeline -- such as major trade unions desperate for jobs for members -- will be less likely to abandon Mr. Obama than would the greenies if he were seen to have caved on Keystone before Nov. 6 . <p> So even with pressure from Mr. Clinton , it may still be a year or more before Keystone gets the go ahead . But with an ever enlarging list of supporters , Keystone will eventually get built . 
@@779349 <h> Starve , Get Aid , Repeat <p> Albert Einstein once said the definition of insanity is doing the same thing over and over again and expecting different results . <p> On Friday , February 3 , the United Nations declared the famine over in Somalia . All is not well yet . Some nine million people throughout the Horn of Africa still face a serious food crisis , but the worst of the disaster that has claimed as many as 100,000 lives -- mostly children -- has passed . <p> Now the world will turn its attention , resources , and donations to the next disaster , like the growing hunger crisis in the Sahel region of northwest Africa . <p> We can predict how it will go . <p> Aid organizations will issue dire press releases for a month or two , which will be ignored at first . As the scale of the emergency grows , the media will discover the story and camera teams will rush to the scene . There will be a flurry of news stories . A burst of donations from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ governments . Depending on the profile of the emergency , after a few weeks -- not much more -- journalists will lose interest and donations will trickle off . <p> Lather , rinse , repeat . <p> We 're caught in Einstein 's loop of insanity , delivering emergency assistance over and over but never getting the result we want : stopping people from dying of hunger . <p> An often-repeated statistic in the development community is that every dollar spent on prevention will save four dollars in emergency response . After the 2005 drought in the Horn of Africa , another study found that it had cost $80 a day to treat a malnourished child , but it would have only cost one dollar a day to prevent the malnutrition in the first place with development programs . <p> We ca n't stop droughts from happening , but we can give communities the tools to survive so they are prepared and do n't starve when the drought strikes . <p> The humanitarian news website AlertNet has released the results of a survey of 41 of the world 's largest international @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 10 per cent or less of their spending goes to projects that help communities prevent or reduce the risk of disasters . Fourteen more NGOs were n't even able get the information to answer the question . In other words , a majority of organizations are not making a meaningful effort to help communities prepare for and survive disasters . <p> Some organizations have made prevention a priority , and have had an impact because of it . In 1984 , the famine in Ethiopia killed more than 400,000 people . Since 1984 , these organizations have helped communities in the region become more prepared and resilient to drought . As a result , this time the drought affected more countries but the death toll was much lower . <p> Twenty-five of those NGOs polled by AlertNet said they would like to increase their disaster prevention work , if they can find the funding . <p> So what 's the problem ? <p> " Funding for disaster risk reduction and disaster preparedness is not very ' sexy ' for donors -- global , domestic and private , " says Jouni Hemberg @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the AlertNet report . <p> The 2011 " Millennial Donors Report , " by U.S. fundraising firm Achieve , found that 77 per cent of people who give to charity would be at least somewhat likely to stop giving money if they did n't know what impact their money was having . When we give money to a charity , we want to know where that money is going , and what difference it is going to make . <p> Delivering food and water to tens of thousands of families , as most organizations do in an emergency , has an obvious impact . When you donate to support emergency relief , you know exactly how your money is going to save lives . <p> Teaching sustainable farming practices or supporting local governments to manage water resources better -- that 's a little more complex and the impact is n't quite so obvious and immediate . Come the next drought , less people will need us to deliver emergency food and water . <p> You can save more lives more effectively by helping communities prepare to survive a drought than by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and people are already dying . <p> However , many organizations are sending donors the exact opposite message with their marketing . <p> In every disaster , all the ads from aid groups say : " NOW is the time to give ! " and " We need your donations more than ever ! " <p> What 's the message behind this urgency ? That it 's more important to give to emergency aid than ongoing development that could prevent the emergency in the first place . <p> Let 's turn it on its head . Why not buy the full-page newspaper ad after the drought , saying now is when we need your donations the most . By giving now , you are ensuring no one starves during the next drought . <p> Aid groups then need to do a much better job of explaining the impact of prevention work to their donors . <p> With the effects of climate change , disasters are only going to become more frequent . In regions like the Horn of Africa , where droughts used to come every five to seven years , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ We will fail if we keep trying to do the same old emergency response and expect different results . <p> Working with communities to prepare for disasters will save more lives , and will be a much more effective use of our aid dollars . 
@@779373 <h> Climate change could wreak havoc on maple syrup industry <p> ( KAWARTHA LAKES ) It has been an unseasonably warm winter this year , which is giving some cause for concern for local maple syrup producers who wonder what the warmer weather will mean for their already unpredictable industry . <p> About 55 people were on hand for the recent annual general meeting of the Halibruton-Kawartha Maple Syrup Producer Association ( HKMSPA ) at the Buckhorn Community Centre . The group is a unit of the Ontario Maple Syrup Producer 's Association , and hosted agroforestry specialist Todd Leuty of the Ontario Ministry of Agriculture , Food and Rural Affairs to speak about the impact of climate change on the maple syrup industry . <p> He told This Week that while researchers in Canada and the U.S. have been following the increase in temperature over the past few decades , it is still difficult to know what affect these changes will have on the maple syrup industry . <p> " The ecological balance in the forest is very interactive so it 's hard to predict what will @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ terms of the maple syrup industry , the effects can be seen as producers have been tapping their trees earlier and earlier every year . <p> " Fewer producers are tapping by the calendar every year , " he said , adding that they are also much more aware of the health of their trees . <p> According to The Maple Daily , a news site dedicated to maple syrup , sugar maple trees now release their sap about 8.2 days earlier in the year and stop producing it 11.4 days earlier , resulting in a total of about 10 per cent loss in the duration of the maple production season . <p> What is clear , Mr. Leuty said , is that over the next 50-100 years , the temperature changes will only increase more rapidly . And while that may seem like a long time , Mr. Leuty said , " for a sugar maple that 's not even half of the life of a tree , if it 's healthy . " <p> As far as this year 's maple syrup production goes , as with every year @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ will be until it is done . <p> " But everyone hopes for a good year , " Mr. Leuty said . <p> " This year it should be better , there is lots of groundwater . " <p> Ideally , for maple syrup production , four to six weeks of freezing nights and thawing days are needed to encourage sap flow and result in a good season . If it is too warm at night production will suffer . <p> Maple syrup producer Carolyn Moore , who has lived near Janetville on 17 acres with her husband Jim for 29 years , said they got into the business about 24 years ago . <p> " We were looking for ways to make a little extra money from the same acreage , so the bush was the obvious choice . " <p> She said the quantities of maple syrup they produce year-to-year vary depending on the weather , so they never know what to expect , but last year was a good one . <p> " Last year was the biggest production we 've ever had , " she said . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the couple , she added that it is very hard to measure because there are so many contributing factors . One year , they were n't able to tap their trees until March 28 , but for most years the tapping takes place between mid-February and mid-April , she said . The amount of syrup tapped can also vary from 0.6 L per tap on their worst year several years ago to 1.4 L per tap last year . <p> But in spite of the uncertainty of the business , Squirrel Creek Farm Ltd. owner Dave Brackenridge of Millbrook , who sells maple syrup producing equipment , said there has been a jump in his equipment sales . <p> " We 've had more interest in the past three or four years because of government programs more than anything else , " he said adding that grant programs are encouraging producers to upgrade old equipment . <p> There is currently a debate as to whether forestry experts should be stepping in and taking action in the form of something like an assisted migration , moving trees to a more northern climate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ but like many other aspects of the industry , there is a lot of uncertainty . <p> " There 's a lot of speculation and guess work right now , " he said . <p> " We need to back up and look at weather data as far back as you can go to get an idea of what is really happening , " he added , which he says is happening right now in Canada and the U.S.

We work to protect Canadian independence by promoting progressive policies on fair trade , clean water , energy security , public health care , and other issues of social and economic concern to Canadians . <h> Offshore drilling in the Gulf of St. Lawrence ? <p> The Canada-Newfoundland and Labrador Offshore Petroleum Board has rejected an attempt by Corridor Resources to limit the impact of an environmental assessment process on their license to drill in the Gulf of St Lawrence ( you can read the decision here ) . <p> A bit of background : <p> Corridor Resources filed a project description for the drilling of an exploration well on their Old Harry prospect @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Old Harry is a disputed boundary region , straddling Quebec and Newfoundland and Labrador . It has been estimated that Old Harry ( see a map here -- scroll down ) holds up to two billion barrels of recoverable oil -- this is twice the size of Hibernia , east of St. John 's N.L. There is also up to 7 trillion cubic feet of natural gas . If tapped , this would be the biggest offshore oil and gas resource in eastern Canada . <p> Quebec has had a moratorium on oil and gas drilling in the Gulf of St Lawrence and estuary in place since 1998 , but this could be in jeopardy upon the completion of a Quebec Strategic Environmental Assessment . <p> After a significant number of public comments were received , the Canada-Newfoundland and Labrador Offshore Petroleum Board asked the federal government to decide on whether the Gulf should be opened up to drilling . The federal government declined , instead proposing an update to an environmental assessment of the western Newfoundland offshore area including public consultations , and a continuation and completion of a separate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it will proceed with public consultations associated with the Environmental Assessment for a drilling program on Corridor Resources exploration licence . The recent decision was spurred by the company 's concern that the consultation would take away time from their exploratory license . <p> We are all familiar with the devastation caused by the BP oil spill in the Gulf of Mexico that began April 20 , 2010 . The impacts of this spill are still very much being felt . The spill spewed more than 4.1 million barrels of crude over 87 days into the Gulf , whose $3 billion fishing industry provides one-third of all seafood consumed in the U.S. As quoted in Reuters , " They destroyed the labor force , " said Louisiana oysterman Terrance M. Shelley , in a Feb , 12 interview . Shelly 's family has 18,000 acres of oyster reefs . " They destroyed the quality of the oysters , and they destroyed the market for Louisiana oysters . " Like New Orleans , Old Harry is surrounded by fisheries rich areas near the shores of Quebec , Newfoundland and Labrador , Nova @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ question , when it comes to offshore drilling , is not whether there will be a spill , but when , and how much devastation it will wreak . The Gulf of St Lawrence also includes a number of popular tourism destinations -- jobs and livelihoods that are at stake if drilling moves ahead . <p> Ecologically sensitive waters , endangered species <p> Old Harry also happens to be an ecologically sensitive area . As reported by the St. Lawrence Coalition , there are numerous endangered species that call it home including the Atlantic Cod , Deepwater Redfish , Leatherback Turtle , Fin Whale , Blue Whale , Roseate Tern , Harlequin Duck and more . It is an extremely fragile ecosystem consisting of spawning sites , growth areas and migratory routes for crustaceans , mollusks , fish and marine mammals . Suffice to say , a significant spill in these waters threatens these species and ecosystems . <p> Canada 's reputation <p> Canada is increasingly being seen as an eco-outlaw , more interested in becoming an energy superpower than ensuring Canadians energy security needs are met and reducing climate change @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of development in the tar sands , is an indicator that we are scrapping the bottom of the barrel , going after increasingly polluting and harder to access , risky resources . Environment Minister Peter Kent 's comments in his August 15 correspondence with CEO of the Canada-Newfoundland and Labrador Offshore Petroleum Board are telling , " I am confident that a screening environmental assessment that will include extensive public consultation can adequately address the issues associated with the project . " ( source ) <p> This is the wrong direction . The climate change crisis is real and requires leadership on the part of governments to reduce emissions in a planned , and equitable manner . <p> The road ahead : <p> The Council of Canadians supports the call for a moratorium on all projects and requests for permits for offshore drilling in the Gulf of St Lawrence . Unlike Minister Kent , we believe an open , democratic process will allow Atlantic Canadians to have their voices heard , leading to right decision -- no offshore drilling . <p> We have formally joined the St. Lawrence Coalition -- visit @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 60 organizations and 2400 individual members that support a moratorium on oil and gas exploration in the Gulf of St Lawrence . You can read a coalition presentation about oil and gas exploration and exploitation in the Gulf of St. Lawrence here . <p> Our chapters have already indicated their intention of participating in the public consultation process . @@781392 <p> Sumeet Gulati is an Associate Professor in the Food and Resource Economics Group at the University of British Columbia . He also maintains a blog on environmental policy , and can be followed on Twitter . <p> I am going to India , one of the world 's fastest-growing economies . Measured by purchasing power parity ( how much it takes to buy a uniform set of goods ) , India 's economy is now the world 's third largest . Is n't now the time for India to take part in the battle against climate change ? <p> Reuters <h> Mobile <h> Look ahead <h> Economy <p> It 's midnight , as Cathay Pacific 695 descends into Delhi . Through the haze its passengers see a brightly lit freeway . Even at this late hour , a constant flow of traffic . The visibility is a distinct improvement over January , when some nights , almost nothing can be seen . I take a taxi from the airport to Hari Nagar , an Ambassador -- a uniquely Indian car -- its body unchanged from a 1956 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , I smell fumes from the engine leaking into the interior , a slightly open window lets in Delhi 's smoky air . Indoors after forty five minutes , I wash my face ; dark water drips from it , my nostrils are black with soot . <p> The next morning , I am on the Shatabdi Express from New Delhi to Chandigarh . Indian Railways , even during a budgetary crisis , lavishes service on its first class customers . Six newspapers are on each seat pair : three English dailies , reporting in the language of the elite ; three in Hindi , the preferred language of the masses . Hindi newspapers ignore the environment . Their pages focus on politics , corruption , income inequality and sport . Based on a study by the Energy and Resources Institute and Unicef , the Times of India reports how a quarter of children living along Delhi 's Yamuna river have more than 10 micrograms of lead in their blood ( widely considered as the threshold for public intervention ) . Lead levels in children exposed to the polluted north Delhi @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Financial Express reports on bans recommended on mining and quarrying in Goa 's ecologically sensitive areas , and near Kaziranga national park , home of India 's one-horn Rhino . India 's richer elite are marginally concerned with the environment ; the poorer , Hindi-speaking majority are not . <p> Just a few weeks ago , Yale University 's Environmental Performance Indicators ( EPI ) for 2012 rated India as having the world 's worst air . Overall , India 's pollution ranked 125 out of 132 countries . Responding to the report , a Department of environment scientist said : " it is a non-issue , we have other pressing problems like poverty . " His comments reflect India 's mood . The poor crave food to survive , India 's growing middle class crave the material trappings of economic growth . <p> As the western world got richer its environment improved . The first reductions were in smog ( the concentration of fine particulates in air ) . A visible , constant reminder to how dirty our air is , smog causes emphysema , bronchitis , asthma and lung @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ developed countries . As its development continued , the western world turned its attention to harmful , but less visible pollutants such as lead . Causing nervous system and kidney damage , lead is particularly damaging to children . Even levels below 10 micrograms impair their cognitive development . Climate change is a more abstract concept . It affects our physical environment , seriously impacts our ecology , causing floods or droughts , but no immediate impact on human health . Even within the developed world , strong action on climate change is lacking . <p> One day into my trip I realize it is unrealistic to expect India to meaningfully address climate change . The country is blanketed in polluted air , and flooded in polluted water . Even in the face of severe health impacts the government presents ineffectual action on local pollutants . Expecting India 's government to incur costs for reducing greenhouse gases is to fool ourselves . We must wait for a will to tackle local pollutants , before action on climate change will occur . <h> Restrictions <p> All rights reserved . @@783755 <h> From glacier to glacier : Nunavut meets Nepal <h> " Looking at the children , I saw myself " <p> The capital city of Kathmandu , Nepal is full of colours , sounds , and smells . ( PHOTO BY HENRY HUNTINGTON ) <p> Laimikie Palluq gets a " tika " at a temple in Kathmandu . The tika represents the " third eye " and the idea of meditation and spiritual enlightenment . ( PHOTO BY HENRY HUNTINGTON <p> David Iqaqrialu and Shari Gearheard dance with their guide Dhawa Lama to traditional music after dinner with their local hosts in Tsum . ( PHOTO BY HENRY HUNTINGTON ) <p> Ask four Nunavummiut from Clyde River about their recent two-week trip to Nepal , and they 'll tell you 10,000-kilometre journey to the Himalayan country was " a trip of a lifetime . " <p> Hunters Laimikie Palluq and David Iqaqrialu , along with Mike Jaypoody , a young filmmaker and computer technician , and University of Colorado researcher Dr. Shari Gearheard travelled to Nepal Feb. 3 to 17 as part of a National Science Foundation-funded research @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ who 's lived in Clyde River for the past eight years -- and her Alaska-based colleague Dr. Henry Huntington , brought local snow and ice experts from Nunavut and Nepal together to talk about shared changes in the environment . <p> That 's because , although far apart , the Arctic Canadian and high-altitude Himalayan regions of Asia both depend upon snow and ice -- and that environment of snow and ice is changing . <p> The impact of climate change was n't the only similarity the Nunavut group noticed between Nepal and Nunavut . <p> After visiting Nepal 's capital city of Kathmandu , seeing sites , and meeting with local environmental organizations and researchers , the group then travelled to the Tsum Valley . <p> There , residents of Tsum live a life close to the land : they farm and herd as their ancestors have done for generations . <p> Tsum , opened to foreigners only since 2008 , is considered to be a sacred valley in Nepal , a place where all life is protected ( it has the special designation of a ' non-killing ' valley @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Nunavut stayed with a local family . <p> And , for the visiting Inuit , life in Tsum reminded them of growing up on the land of northern Baffin Island , before there were any settlements . <p> " Looking at the children , I saw myself , " said Iqaqrialu , referring to the small children he saw carrying firewood , helping with lifestock , or doing other chores in the village . <p> " I used to help my father just like they are doing . " <p> The Nunavummiut also saw how many of the environmental changes occurring in Tsum are similar to those experienced in Nunavut , during meetings with the people who live in the Tsum Valley . <p> " They are seeing many of the same changes , such as rapidly receding glaciers , changes in weather patterns , and some observations of new birds and animals , " said Gearheard . <p> And , like in Nunavut , it 's not only the physical environment that 's changing . <p> Tsum residents talked about social changes they face , such as a more rapid @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the valley is only now getting electricity , satellite television access , and tourism ) . <p> A proposed road through the valley also creates worries about what changes may come along with that project . <p> Now that they 're back from Nepal the Clyde River travellers hope to share what they learned with their community and other Nunavummiut . <p> Jaypoody , who brought along his video cameras on the trip , is now editing the footage for a film . <p> Jaypoody and the others plan to show their videos and photos at a community gathering in Clyde River , to give their friends and family at home a better idea of what they saw and learned in Nepal . <p> Visiting the far-away country has helped them all appreciate what they have at home and the special aspects of their own culture . <p> " I will be talking about this to everyone , for a long time , " said Iqaqrialu , also an instructor at the Piqqusilirivik Cultural School in Clyde River . <p> With files from Shari Gearheard <p> : Laimikie Palluq and Shari @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ people of Tsum Valley in Nepal . ( PHOTO BY HENRY HUNTINGTON ) 
@@785071 <h> Global warming could spell the end of Canada 's outdoor hockey rink <p> A team of Canadian climate scientists is predicting the widespread disappearance of outdoor hockey rinks across the country in the next 50 years due to global warming -- with some regions of the sport 's spiritual birthplace likely to witness an even earlier eclipse of old-time shinny on natural ice . <p> " The ability to skate and play hockey outdoors is a critical component of Canadian identity and culture , " three researchers from Montreal 's McGill and Concordia universities write in the latest issue of the U.K.-based scholarly journal Environmental Research Letters . <p> " Wayne Gretzky learned to skate on a backyard skating rink ; our results imply that such opportunities may not be available to future generations of Canadian children . " <h> Related <p> The scientists -- Nikolay Damyanov and Lawrence Mysak of McGill and Concordia 's Damon Matthews -- tracked historical temperature trends recorded since 1951 at 142 meteorological stations in every corner of the country . <p> They also interviewed outdoor icemakers in Quebec and Ontario to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ an open-air rink and keep it usable for the duration of the skating season . <p> The researchers learned that , in general , it takes three consecutive days with a maximum temperature of -5 C to make ice and begin the outdoor skating season . <p> The length of the season is more difficult to pin down , since a rink 's annual life span depends on the local flooding and maintenance regime as well as weather conditions . But the researchers set the same minus-5 C standard to tally the approximate number of days when outdoor rinks should have been available for skaters and hockey players . <p> " Many individual locations have seen a statistically significant decrease in the number of viable ice-flooding days , suggesting a significant shortening of the length of the outdoor skating season over much of the country between 1951 and 2005 , " the published paper states , identifying B.C. , southern Alberta and the Prairies as the areas with the sharpest drop in the number of game days . <p> The historical warming effect was noticeable but not as significant in Ontario @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , overall , no significant change in the durability of outdoor rinks over the past half-century . <p> Although the researchers did not make detailed calculations projecting the temperature trends into the future , they observed that continued winter warming -- as predicted for Canada in standard climate-change models -- will result in fewer and fewer viable locations for outdoor rinks in Canada and shorter and shorter seasons in most places where skating persists . <p> Referring to southern B.C. and Alberta in particular , the researchers stated : " In the most extreme case of the Southwest Canada region , a simple linear extrapolation of the OSS ( outdoor skating season ) length trend from the last 30 years of our record into the future shows that the number of viable rink-flooding days could reach zero by mid-century . " <p> Matthews told Postmedia News that the research team knows of no other study assessing the state of outdoor rinks in Canada or elsewhere around the world . <p> But it was " an obvious thing " to examine , he said , because " hockey culture in Canada is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ people in the country it 's their only exercise and the centre point of winter culture . " <p> The study 's conclusions echo concerns expressed anecdotally in recent years by many outdoor icemakers . Meanwhile , a number of municipalities across Canada have been investing in refrigerated outdoor skating facilities that remain open in warmer weather . <p> Although the researchers did not assess the viability trends for ponds , rivers and other water bodies as skating surfaces , they noted in a summary of their study that the Rideau Canal experienced one of its shortest skating seasons ever this winter . <p> Organizers of Ottawa-Gatineau 's annual Winterlude festival , highlighted by activities along the canal 's famous skateway , also made a point this year of expanding the number and range of events held at other venues because of concerns about milder temperatures in recent years . <p> Matthews said he and his fellow researchers " knew this would be an issue that would resonate with Canadians , " and called it a " fortuitous coincidence " that Prime Minister Stephen Harper is a well-known " hockey enthusiast @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for outdoor hockey rinks might " push the Conservative government to take action " to curb Canada 's contribution to global carbon dioxide emissions , widely seen by scientists as a key driver of climate change .

@@785116 <h> Study says outdoor ice hockey could perish in some areas of Canada <h> Outdoor hockey could perish <p> Hamilton Spectator File Photo <p> The Hamilton Bulldogs and the Toronto Marlies meet in the 2012 AHL Outdoor Classic at Ivor Wynne Stadium in Hamilton in January . Outdoor hockey is on its way to being an indoor-only sport in some areas of Canada , a new study warns . <p> Canada 's favourite pastime is on its way to being an indoor-only sport in some areas of the country , a new study warns . <p> The study , released Monday by United Kingdom-based IOP Publishing , says outdoor ice hockey in Canada is being threatened by climate change . <p> Lawrence Mysak , coauthor of the report and a professor at McGill University in Montreal , Que. , said warmer winter temperatures caused by climate change is restricting the operation of ice rinks . <p> " We were able to see that in general , the rinks were being opened later and later over the last ... 50 years , and secondly that the length of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sometimes three weeks , " said Mysak from his office in Montreal last week . <p> Mysak fears the gradual warming of the earth caused by burning fossil fuels could eventually cause the beloved outdoor activity to perish in several decades . <p> " If we extend the trends into the future , there could be no outdoor skating rinks with global warming taking place , " said Mysak , who grew up carving his skates into outdoor rinks in Edmonton , Alta . <p> Regions that are being hit the hardest are the Prairies , southeastern British Columbia and southern Ontario and Quebec . The Maritimes and northern parts of the country did not see significant changes , he said . <p> Using historical weather data from more than 140 weather stations across Canada since the 1950s , the researchers calculated the annual start date and length of the outdoor skating season , said Mysak . <p> He said the beginning of the season is considered the last of three days where the maximum temperature does not exceed -5 C , as it takes several cold days to lay the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at how many days in January and February were cold enough to skate on back yard or community rinks built on the ground or snow , said Mysak . <p> Damon Matthews , coauthor of the report and a professor at Concordia University in Montreal , Que. , said many regions experienced decreases in the length of the outdoor skating season . <p> " It 's hard to imagine parts of the country that currently do enjoy outdoor skating not being able to do that at some point in the future , " said Matthews , adding the study is the first of its kind . <p> " Outdoor skating , particularly hockey , is synonymous with Canadian culture . I think there 's big cultural ramification to this as well . " <p> But Justin Beaulieu of Lower Sackville , N.S. , said losing outdoor skating rinks is a small casualty in the grand scheme of climate change . <p> " There 's a lot more than skating that we have to worry about with climate change , " said the 39-year-old who was enjoying his Sunday on the ice @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It 's the big picture . We have a lot more to worry about . " <p> In order to ensure Canada 's future hockey stars will have access to back yard rinks , the country must tackle reducing greenhouse gases , the experts agree . <p> Since 1950 , winter temperatures in Canada have increased by more than 2.5 C -- three times the globally averaged warming attributed to global warming caused by human activity , a news release from the publisher said . <p> Mysak said the study began as a 2011 thesis by McGill masters student Nikolay Damyanov. 
@@787183 <h> Canada 's stifled federal scientists <p> Speakers at a seminar at the recent Vancouver meeting of the American Association for the Advancement of Science criticized the Conservative government for preventing government scientists from speaking openly to the press . <p> " The more controversial the story , the less likely you are to talk to the scientists , " said Margaret Munro , a science journalist with the Postmedia Network and a speaker at the meeting . According to the CBC , Gary Goodyear , the minister of state for Science and Technology , did not respond at all . <p> Government scientists have to go through several layers of approval before an interview can be granted . Sometimes this goes as high as the Privy Council Office , which advises the Prime Minister . These requests take a long time and are often rejected , which effectively takes the scientists out of the news cycle , according to Munro . <p> According to the BBC , the " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ introduced by the government in 2008 . <p> The protocol says : " Just as we have one department we should have one voice . Interviews sometimes present surprises to ministers and senior management . Media relations will work with staff on how best to deal with interview requests . This should include asking the programme expert to respond with approved lines . " <p> The most prominent recent case of alleged muzzling came in 2011 , when Kristi Miller , who runs a salmon genetics project at the federal Pacific Biological Station on Vancouver Island , was barred from speaking to the press about her research into unusual mortality rates in Fraser River sockeye salmon . The journal Science notified over 7,400 journalists around the world about the published research , which identified Miller as the principal author and press contact . But , when the press called for interviews , the Privy Council Office turned them down . <p> Fisheries and Oceans Canada , speaking to the BBC , maintained that it is not muzzling scientists : " The Department works daily to ensure it provides the public with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ policies , programs , services and initiatives , in accordance with the Federal Government 's Communications Policy . " However , they did not allow Miller to speak to the BBC , though Miller confirmed she would have done the interview if she had been given permission . <p> The other major complaint was from late 2011 and dealt with the contributions of Environment Canada scientist David Tarasick to a paper showing unprecedented ozone loss in the Arctic . Environment Canada did not allow Tarasick to speak to the press about the work in a timely manner . <p> " I suspect the federal government would prefer that its scientists do n't discuss research that points out just how serious the climate change challenge is , " said Thomas Pedersen , a professor at the University of Victoria . <p> Five Canadian journalists ' organizations and the World Federation of Science Journalists have jointly written an open letter to Prime Minister Stephen Harper criticizing the government 's media policy with respect to science . " NOAA scientists are allowed to give their own opinions in interviews , so long as it is clear that they are not speaking for the NOAA . <p> " It 's the dream policy , " said Andrew Weaver , a University of Victoria scientist and one of the speakers at the AAAS meeting . " It 's kind of the gold , the platinum standard by which everyone else will be judged . " 
@@787275 <p> There 's a great line in the 1987 Diane Keaton movie , Baby Boom , when her character finds out her well has run dry . She says something to the effect of : " I want to turn on my tap and have water come out . I do n't want to know where it comes from . " <p> I 've been thinking a lot about that line since the night a few weeks ago when I turned on my tap while making dinner ... and nothing came out . We live in the country and draw the water to our house from a well . It has n't run dry , though it turns out we have several other water-related problems that remain unresolved -- mainly because it is winter and the ground is frozen solid , making digging an impossibility . <p> We have lived in the country for many years so we are used to water problems , though we were caught unprepared in this case with no water in reserve . I can assure you that will not happen again . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ indoor plumbing anywhere near as loudly and fervently as they should . Living without it all of a sudden is a little like breaking your collarbone -- you know in theory that the bone is important , but you do n't understand how much you rely on it for almost every physical movement until you ca n't use it any more . <p> Try living without running water , even for just one day . Forget your morning shower . Forget flushing your toilet , brushing your teeth , making coffee , cleaning clothes or dishes . Washing your hands . Watering your plants . Rinsing your vegetables . To say nothing of drinking a glass of the stuff . You do n't realize how often you reach for that tap until it stops working . <p> A lot of us -- including me -- are like Diane Keaton 's character . We just want to turn on our taps . We do n't want to know where the water comes from . <p> I like to think of our family now as true practitioners of Zen . We chop wood @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ access to a pump about 30 metres away , down a fairly steep hill . We carry 20 litres at a time in plastic jugs . If you 're having trouble visualizing that , here 's something that might help : It weighs about 40 pounds . <p> According to Environment Canada , the average Canadian runs through approximately 329 litres of water per day . There are five people living in our house at the moment , including three teenaged boys who love to shower , so that would bring our grand total to 1,645 litres per day . Right now we are using 100 . In total . <p> The number is skewed slightly because it does n't take into account that many of us are away from the house during the day and using toilets elsewhere . It discounts our visits to the laundromat , to public washroom facilities when they happen to present themselves , and to the community centre for the occasional hot shower . But this experience is teaching me that I could easily survive on a lot less water than I normally use . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ about eight litres of water to adequately flush a toilet . Older toilets use 20 litres per flush , a phenomenal waste . I can assure you , you would think twice about that if you had to carry the 20 litres every time you went to the bathroom . <p> The average shower consumes anywhere from 56 to 95 litres of water . These days we are managing to get relatively clean with some soap , a washcloth and a bowl of warm water -- about one litre in total . No shampoo . No lingering . It 's not ideal , but it works . <p> This does n't sound like fun , does it ? It is n't . At first it was like camping -- kind of amusing , with an end in sight . That was a month ago . With snow on the ground and the temperature still below zero , there is no end in sight . It is not like camping ; it 's beginning to feel permanent . And nothing about it is even remotely funny . <p> I know our water @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ n't help but make the connection to something bigger and more disturbing than a mere household inconvenience . One day this little inconvenience might threaten to become a nationwide reality . One day our water might run out . And there will be no laundromat to rely upon , no community centre showers or public washrooms . <p> It 's time to start imagining what this might feel like . We waste water . We pollute it . Climate change is already having an effect on how much water we will have access to in the future . And Canada is a water-rich country . Many other places in the world are already suffering what we can not yet envision . <p> Everyone knows this . We 've all heard it a thousand times . Yet still we water our lawns and wash our cars obsessively . You can not appreciate the gift of running water until you experience what your life is like without it . I wonder how it would affect people if Canada instituted a water day , like Earth Hour when everyone shuts off their lights . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for an entire day -- I wonder how we might feel the next time we ran our taps for no reason . <p> I have already vowed this experience will change the way our family consumes water . I am looking into low-flow toilets and shower heads . I am making peace with my dirty car . And I will be saying a small prayer of gratitude from now on every time I turn on my tap .

@@789269 <h> Mobile Leaderboard <h> Modern life in the Arctic showcased in new doc <p> It 's not about science or arts . People of a Feather as awarded Best Environmental Film at the Vancouver International Film Festival and " Best BC Film at the Vancouver Film Critics Circle . <p> Canadian ecologist and former UBC student , Joel Heath , first set foot in the Arctic as a member of the Canadian Wildlife Service to investigate a mass die-out of eider ducks in the late 1990s . <p> Heath described the film as " artistic science . " <p> People of a Feather is screening until Friday , March 9 at Denman Theatre , and then from 10 to 13 at RIO Theatre . 
@@789278 <h> Lorne Gunter : For climate cues , look to the sun <p> Ottawa 's giant skating rink on the Rideau Canal was closed in February due to thin ice caused by unseasonably mild temperatures . Yet , at the same time , ice blocked the canals of Venice for the first time in recent memory as temperatures in the exquisite Italian city dropped to -10C for more than a week . In the Netherlands , canals were closed to commercial boat traffic because ice made them unnavigable -- another unusual development . <p> Also in early February , fountains in southern France froze over . Polish rail lines were chocked with metres of snow . Swiss villages were cut off by record accumulations this winter . In Japan , tens of thousands of residents were confined to their homes because there was too little removal equipment to clear all the white stuff . At one point three weeks ago , more than 140,000 people worldwide were reportedly stranded by snow . <p> So which is likely to be the new norm : North America 's mild winter @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ season ? <p> To hear climate alarmists and environmentalists tell it , the world will soon be without winter . There will be no more backyard skating rinks or Arctic sea ice to sustain the polar bears . Snow will become a rarity in much of Europe , and tornados such as the ones that devastated large swaths of the American Midwest last weekend will become more commonplace . <p> But that 's not what some solar physicists are predicting . <h> Related <p> Scientists who have made careers of studying the sun warn that our star is about to enter a less-active phase -- a solar minimum that could last 30 years or longer . If that happens , some physicists see a worldwide return to the temperatures of the Little Ice Age ( LIA ) . Not coincidentally , the deepest part of the LIA -- during the late 17th century -- was the last time our sun generated as few sunspots and as little geomagnetic activity as it appears set to generate for the next few decades . <p> Solid records of the connection between solar activity and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ If so-called proxy records are included -- evidence from tree rings and ice-core samples , for instance -- then the connection is thousands of years old . <p> The sun-temperature connection only makes sense . Which is warmer , summer or winter ? Daytime or night ? A sunny day or a cloudy one ? <p> Sometimes I wonder whether our Neolithic ancestors understood better than modern climate alarmists what warmed the Earth . They did n't build monuments that marked the summer and winter solstices because they worried the soot from their cooking fires was dangerously warming the planet . They built Stonehenge and the Goseck Circle and others to ensure the declining sun of winter would come back and prompt the return of spring and the plants and animals they relied on for their subsistence . <p> For years , now , the global-warming establishment has tried to minimize the effects the sun has on weather and climate . For instance , rough drafts of the UN 's next five-year report on climate change ( which are already circulating ) apparently devote just a single sentence to the sun @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Earth , while page after page after page obsesses on the **26;252;TOOLONG theory . <p> The fact is , scientists have studied the sun so thoroughly for so long that they can forecast with about 85% confidence what will happen to our temperatures if the number of sunspots rises or lowers from one cycle to the next and if the sun 's geomagnetic activity strengthens or weakens . They even know the effect on temperatures if one solar cycle -- typically about 11 years -- is longer or shorter than the cycle before it . And by studying the forces at work deep inside the sun , they can estimate with accuracy the length of the next cycle or two . This gives them a good idea of the sun 's influence on climate for the next few decades . <p> According to a recent study by three Norwegian scientists -- Jan-Erik Solheim , Kjell Stordahl and Ole Humlum -- the sun 's current cycle has lasted so long that the next , due to begin any time now , will see a decline in temperatures of 0.63C . And that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ after that will witness a temperature drop of 0.95C . <p> Given that the planet has warmed only about 0.7C or 0.8C over the past century , that means all the warming Earth has experienced since 1900 could be wiped out in the next solar cycle , and in the cycle after that temperatures could retreat to levels not seen since the 18th century . <p> Start idling your full-sized SUVs in your driveways now . The planet may need all the global warming it can get .

@@793531 <h> On energy and environment , Pembina plays both offense and defense <h> Pembina Institute plays offense and defense on environment <p> Ed Whittingham , executive director of the Pembina Institute , gets upset when they call him a " foreign-funded eco-radical beholden to foreign funders . " While the Calgary-based Pembina Institute has for years been one of the Canadian oil sands ' toughest critics and gets some funding from U.S. foundations , it 's also a big consultant to the sector . Corporations account for half its $5-million a year in revenue . The unique model is driven by Mr. Whittingham , a clean-cut , business-suited environmentalist with an International MBA from York University 's Schulich School of Business . He enjoys hunting and is a captivating public speaker , a talent honed dabbling in theatre , including for an improv group in the 1990s called the Flukes of Nature . Mr. Whittingham spoke to Claudia Cattaneo , the Financial Post 's Western Business Columnist , about how Canada 's leading energy and environment research organization reconciles playing offense and defense on the environment at the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ do ? <p> A. Our consulting started in the 1990s . Pembina had an environmental group that was working on oil and gas issues , and we were asked by companies , regulators , to review environmental assessments . Along the way we said : If we want to do a good job of this , we need to actually treat it like a professional services firm , and you are going to pay us to review it , and we are going to give you a detailed critique of what we see as the strengths and weaknesses of it , and we will go into bilateral negotiations with you to try to come up with resolutions on whatever the issues are , and then together we will take that to the regulator , and say : This is an example of a company with a public interest stakeholder coming up with resolutions in advance of approval . And from those origins we grew a consulting practice that is half of our people , half of our revenue , that provides advice on a range of sustainable energy issues to federal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 500 companies . We work with most of the oil majors in Calgary -- Suncor , Shell , Nexen , Statoil , ConocoPhillips , CNRL , Devon . We work with renewable energy developers <p> Q.Is most of your business oil and gas related ? <p> A. It 's energy related . Our consulting spans the engineering side of energy , such as conducting a lifecycle value assessment on proposed energy infrastructure , or trying to find out the full range of environmental and social dimensions and working with the developer to redesign it to minimize the negative environmental impacts or maximize positive social impacts . And we will compete with engineering firms for that kind of work , like Golder ( Associates ) and Stantec ( Inc . ) . We also provide advice to companies on management practices and strategies for sustainable energy . <h> Related <p> A. It 's very unique . Among environmental groups I do n't know of any other that has a consulting practice like ours . For us , it 's part of our strategy . We use two approaches . One is policy @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ groups use . But the other is a consulting , hands-on approach . By doing that we understand issues not just at the board level or the CEO level , but also at the engineering shop level . We have developed unparalleled technical expertise , on what technologies we need to for instance drive down emissions . I can retain engineers -- and 28% of our people are engineers -- we develop relationships with industry and governments and we can use to bring parties together . Pembina does a lot of convening behind the scenes , whether it 's companies and environmental groups , so we make the same asks on climate change policies , or whether it 's Pembina working with 13 municipalities in B.C. to change building codes to encourage green buildings . <p> Q.Do your corporate clients take your advice seriously ? <p> A. Oh , sure . And if you look at the lifecycle value assessment work we did for Suncor , or for Cameco , the uranium miner in Saskatchewan , they made material design changes on everything from an upgrader , to location of a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ work that we have done . <p> Q.What about conflict of interest -- how can you on the one hand advise these companies and take money from them , and on the other hand criticize their strategies in the public domain ? <p> A. It 's the same as if we were consulting for a provincial government and a federal government whose policies we sometimes disagree with . I treat it like we are all mature adults . It 's like you have a friendship with someone . You do n't always agree with that person but you can compartmentalize that disagreement , and that does n't preclude us from having a productive , collaborative relationship over here . So , like adults , we agree to disagree on that point , but then let 's really work together on this opportunity . So one example could be , Pembina is concerned about pace and scale of development in the oil sands , and most companies do n't agree with us . But we totally agree with each other that we need to drive down the environmental intensity of the oil @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ work together and figure out how we can do that . <p> Q.You are one of the organizations that receives funding from foreign foundations , an area that has been controversial lately . How large is and why do you take the money ? <p> A. About 10% of revenue comes from foreign organizations . That 's why I get upset when people say , ' you are a foreign-funded eco-radical beholden to foreign funders . ' No , If you look at the revenue , and you think that 30% of our revenue comes from the Canadian corporate sector , I am more likely to be beholden to the Canadian corporate sector than I am beholden to big U.S. foundations . But for us that model , where we have consulting revenue , we have revenue from individual donors , and revenue from foundations -- Canadian and U.S. -- government departments , makes us beholden to no one , because our revenue is diverse and we do n't have one master that we have to answer to . <p> Q.Have you ever found yourself in a situation where you were @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and you were forced to say : ' I will stop doing this work because it 's against our values ? ' <p> A. Sure , and it goes the other way , too . Coming back to the mature adults analogy , let 's say a regulatory hearing is coming up , with a company that is a client , but we are also an intervenor at that hearing . For all involved , we might say : ' Let 's take a time-out for six months and let 's this hearing play out and let 's get back to working in the productive collaborative space . It 's not done out of spite . It 's done out of recognizing the reality , and especially when things are said publicly . <p> Q.The environmental movement is pretty good at presenting a united front . So how do other organizations that have moved into oil sands advocacy , for example the U.S. Natural Resources Defense Council that has been aggressively anti-oil sands , see you ? <p> A. The other groups that are working on oil sands issues see @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ worked on oil sands in a variety of capacities , including consulting around the oil sands for 20 years , and 30% of our staff are engineers who can really dig into the technologies . So they see us as a resource , and they will come and say , ' Pembina , can you help us understand technical issues ? Groups like NRDC , who are essentially a bunch of lawyers , do n't understand the technology the way we do , so they will ask us for advice regularly and we provide it . <p> Q.There is some discussion about whether or not environmental organizations deserve to claim they are charities , which gives them a preferential tax status . Does your revenue from consulting work make you a for-profit group ? <p> A. We think of ourselves as an entrepreneurial not-for-profit . We have , by design , set up this consulting business for strategic reasons , to diversify our revenues so we are not beholden to anyone , but also to make sure that we are alive and well when you need a strong voice for sustainable @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not-for-profits to try to be more entrepreneurial and come up with different lines of revenue , we are a living example of that . 
@@795989 <p> A pickup truck leads a haul vehicle at an oil sands mine near Fort McMurray , Alta. , July 9 , 2008 . Researchers doubt that oilsands-damaged land can be restored to what it was . ( Jeff McIntosh/THE CANADIAN PRESS/Jeff McIntosh/THE CANADIAN PRESS ) <p> A pickup truck leads a haul vehicle at an oil sands mine near Fort McMurray , Alta. , July 9 , 2008 . Researchers doubt that oilsands-damaged land can be restored to what it was . ( Jeff McIntosh/THE CANADIAN PRESS/Jeff McIntosh/THE CANADIAN PRESS ) <h> Rebuilding land destroyed by oil-sands may not restore it , researchers say Add to ... <p> In a small corner of the vast scrape the oilsands have left on northern Alberta , a small sampling of seeds is gradually warming up in the slow boreal spring . <p> Painstakingly hand-gathered last fall from sedges , grasses and shrubs at undisturbed marshes and bogs , the seeds were carefully strewn atop a former Syncrude tailings pond , now a crucial pilot project in wetland reclamation . If they sprout , much will rest on those slender @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and bog birch . <p> There 's the fate of a huge ecosystem being disrupted at an increasing pace . There 're the millions of dollars companies have spent studying how to rebuild wetlands destroyed by oil-sands mining . And there 's the social licence of an industry that promises to restore land -- in the words of one ad -- to " where you 'd never know there 'd been a mine in the first place . " <p> " The probability of success is extremely high , " said Warren Zubot , a Syncrude senior engineer who 's working on the Sandhills project , the rebuilt fen that is home to those scattered seeds . <p> " I 'm very confident that we have the ability to create reclaimed wetlands , " said Christine Daly , wetland reclamation director for Suncor , which has restored a marsh and is working on a fen project of its own . <p> But academic researchers point out at least half the region 's wetlands will be permanently lost . They say millions of tonnes of carbon will be released . <p> Stable @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is unpredictable , while hundreds of square kilometres of pristine bogs , marshes and fens are slated to be torn up at a pace that far outstrips reclamation . <p> " It makes us angry because they will put some kind of plants back on the landscape , but it will not look the way it was and it will not have the same type of functions , " said Suzanne Bayley , a University of Alberta biologist who has been studying the region for nearly two decades . <p> " Thinking that wetlands , or in ( the oil-sands ) case , those peatlands are going to go back to natural states , it 's basically unlikely , " said David Moreno-Mateos , a biologist at the University of California Berkeley who recently published an analysis of 621 restored wetlands around the world . <p> " We do n't know the right way to bring them back . " <p> About 60 per cent of the boreal forest that overlays the oil-sands is in fact composed of wetlands , the most common type of which is a fen . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ vast amounts of water , moderating both flood and drought . They host complex plant and animal communities . The plant matter they accumulate turns into huge reserves of carbon in the form of peat . <p> Ms. Bayley said more than 1,900 square kilometres of fens sit atop mineable oil-sands . Even though current estimates suggest only about 750 square kilometres will be restored as self-sustaining wetlands , that 's still one of the largest reclamation efforts in history -- and one of the most difficult , she suggested . <p> " Wetland reclamation is hugely complex . " <p> Wetlands have been forming either artificially or naturally on oilsands leases for years . The results , said Ms. Bayley , are not encouraging . <p> She and her colleague Rebecca Rooney compared 20 reclaimed oilsands wetlands that were an average of 16 years old to 25 undisturbed sites in the area . The scientists found 70 per cent of the reclaimed sites were in poor ecological health : lower biodiversity , less-productive plants and more land exposed to erosion . <p> Those findings echo Mr. Moreno-Mateos 's research . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ around the world averaged about three-quarters of what they would if undisturbed -- even after a century . And the colder the climate , the slower the recovery . <p> " Alberta is so cold , " he said . " Things will take a lot more time . " <p> Then there 's the carbon . <p> University of Alberta water ecologist David Schindler recently discussed the impact of oil-sands wetland disturbance on climate change-causing greenhouse gas emissions with a Senate committee . <p> He pointed to research conducted by Ms. Rooney that concluded wetlands destroyed in creating the 10 existing and approved oil-sands mines will release between 11 million and 47 million tonnes of carbon . Reclaimed wetlands will begin absorbing carbon again , but their smaller extent means the annual uptake will be up to 7,200 tonnes less . <p> Water quality is a critical issue . <p> Both Syncrude 's and Suncor 's fens will be primarily fed by fresh water . But at Syncrude , some water will seep into the fen from mine tailings below . Rain and snow watering Suncor 's fen will have @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ bitumen mine . That sand will probably contain salts and residual chemicals . <p> Although some natural fens are naturally salty , Ms. Bayley found the more " process-affected " water reclaimed wetlands received , the worse they did . Other studies have concluded the same for ducks , amphibians and fish . <p> Finally , there 's unpredictability . Despite the best work of hydrogeologists and biochemists , Mother Nature has her own ideas . Mr. Moreno-Mateos found that reclaimed wetlands developed different plant and animal communities than natural wetlands . <p> " From what we have found , those wetlands are going to follow their own trajectory , in most of the cases different from what you had before . " <p> Johnathan Price of the University of Waterloo , who helped design Suncor 's pilot fen project , acknowledges there are unknowns . <p> " As with all reclamation , it does n't always turn out as you plan ... As to how long it takes for this to evolve , that is possibly decades . I do n't know . " <p> Still , Mr. Price said @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reclaimed sites may not duplicate what existed before , but they 'll be close . <p> " We do n't have a crystal ball , but we 're hopeful that we 'll get some good plant community ... that is characteristic of some of the reference systems in the area . " <p> Mr. Price expects the Suncor site to develop good plant cover within a few years . <p> Ms. Bayley acknowledges that , over time , wetlands can be reclaimed . But reclaimed as what exactly ? <p> " You ca n't just say , ' They 're going to make it look natural . ' They have to measure . " <p> Current government regulations stipulate that reclaimed landscapes be self-sustaining , solid enough to support traffic and able to play at least some of the environmental roles that they did before they were disturbed . <p> Alberta Environment is only starting to discuss more specific mitigation requirements , said department spokeswoman Carrie Sancartier . <p> Tracy Scott of Ducks Unlimited is participating in those discussions . He said Alberta is moving toward a policy that measures wetland @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ or carbon storage . <p> The department is evaluating 18 different ways to measure those functions . <p> " We 're not very far down this particular road , " Mr. Scott said , although he added the department considers developing that policy a top priority . <p> In the meantime , oil-sands development continues apace . Included in Mr. Schindler 's presentation was a graph showing the gap between disturbed land and reclamation increasing at an exponential rate . <p> " The main problem is how they 've let development get ahead of reclamation , " said Ms. Rooney . " It looks like it 's going to continue growing in that manner . <p> " We currently have no evidence that we really know how to successfully reclaim , but we 're continuing to allow tens of thousands of hectares to be disturbed on the promise that , ' Oh , we 'll be able to put it back . " '

@@796006 <p> rabble blogs are the personal pages of some of Canada 's most insightful progressive activists and commentators . All opinions belong to the writer ; however , writers are expected to adhere to our guidelines . We welcome new bloggers -- contact us for details . <h> Activist Communiqu ? <p> Krystalline Kraus is an intrepid journalist and veteran reporter for rabble.ca since its 2001 beginnings . She needs neither a red cape nor safety goggles to fly into her latest political assignment . She often live-tweets from events -- almost exclusively First Nations and environmental issues . You can follow her on Twitter @krystallinek . <p> In April 2010 , an historical moment occurred . More than 32,000 people , including Indigenous Peoples , social movements , small farmers and some world governmental leaders , converged in Cochabamba , Bolivia for the World People 's Conference on Climate Change and the Rights of Mother Earth . <p> Two outcomes of this conference were the Cochabamba Peoples Accord and the Universal Declaration on the Rights of Mother Earth . The Accord and Declaration gave voice to peoples @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ its many accompanying issues , including depletion of freshwater and other natural resources and the problems of food security , poverty and environmental crises , along with the financial meltdown within the United States and globally . <p> During the Cochabamba world conference , President Evo Morales of Bolivia officially proposed that the United Nations adopt a declaration that recognizes that Nature or " Mother Earth " has certain inherent rights that we humans must respect and defend . The adoption by the United Nations and national and local governments of the Universal Declaration of the Rights of Mother Earth would expand the class of holders of legal rights and would initiate a global process of transformation . <p> Our prophecies and teachings tell us that life on Mother Earth is in danger and is coming to a time of great transformation . As Indigenous Peoples , we are accepting the responsibility designated by our prophecies to tell the world that we must live in peace with each other and the Earth to ensure harmony within Creation . <p> Our Indigenous life-ways are the original " green economies . " This @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is the source of life . Water is her lifeblood . The well-being of the natural environment predicts the physical , mental , emotional and spiritual longevity of our Peoples . <p> Mother Earth 's health and that of our Indigenous Peoples are intrinsically intertwined . When our homelands are in a state of good health our Peoples are truly healthy . This inseparable relationship must be respected for the sake of our future generations and for the well-being of the Earth herself . <p> Alliances are being formed , globally of Indigenous and non-indigenous groups and individuals committed to creating a system of jurisprudence that sees and treats nature and Mother Earth as a fundamental , rights bearing entity . A paradigm , that is based on Indigenous thought and philosophy needs to be forwarded which grants equal rights to nature and which honors the interrelation in all life . <p> This is the greatest challenge facing humanity in the 21st century . How do we re-orientate the dominant industrialized societies so that they pursue human well-being in a manner that contributes to the health of our Mother Earth instead @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ we live in harmony with Nature ? <p> A three-day conference has been scheduled at Haskell Indian Nations University , in Lawrence , Kansas , April 4-6 , 2012 with Indigenous Peoples together from the North and Global South to learn more and to have a discourse about this Rights of Mother Earth , Rights of Nature movement . <p> We invite humanity to come together to improve our collective human behavior so that we may develop a more sustainable world . We can preserve , protect , and fulfill our sacred duties to live with respect in this wonderful Creation . We have the power and responsibility for change .

@@797378 <h> Opossums spreading across GTA <h> Move over , city raccoon . Opossums are the new trash-loving scavenger in town . <p> An opossum is shown making the rounds in Parkdale , south of Queen St. W. <p> By:Niamh ScallanStaff Reporter , Published on Mon Mar 12 2012 <p> Move over , city raccoon . There 's a new trash-loving scavenger in town . <p> Decades after the opossum first wandered north of the border in search of warm weather and garbage aplenty , the furry , long-snouted creature has become somewhat of a fixture in Toronto 's animal kingdom . <p> The number of opossums living in the GTA is unknown . But anecdotally , more and more of the animals -- the only marsupials , pouched mammals , in North America -- have been spotted across the region in recent years , according to Toronto Animal Services supervisor Mary Lou Leiher . <p> Believed to have originated in South America , the opossum began to move northward through the United States in the 1950s , a movement largely motivated by climate change , says Jeff Bowman @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> " It 's pretty clear based on data from opossum studies that they would expand to the north with warming climate , " Bowman said , noting that opossums will likely continue to trek further northward as temperatures continue to rise . <p> Though much of the year proves habitable for the GTA 's opossum population , the whims of Mother Nature are often cruel for the bare-tailed beast , unable to adapt to frigid temperature drops in winter months . <p> " They do still struggle with frostbite on ears and the tips of their tails , " said Nathalie Karvonen , executive director of the Toronto Wildlife Centre which treats dozens of opossums every year for frostbite . <p> Opossums can reach the size of a large house cat and are often confused with rats given their bare , scaly tail and long , whiskery snouts . Known for their nocturnal scavenging for anything from road kill to garbage to fruits and seeds , the animals share many similarities with nuisance-prone raccoons . <p> " Usually , what we hear is that people just do n't know @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ what to expect with them , " Karvonen said , adding that such encounters sometimes spark unwarranted fear and concern . <p> " They are harmless and beneficial , " she said . " They clean up animals that die . " <p> William Krause , a University of Missouri anatomy professor who has researched opossums for biomedical studies since 1967 , called them one of the most misunderstood , and underrated , creatures on the continent . <p> " People are a bit more tolerant today but the prejudices are still there . They really are n't the fearsome animal they 're assumed to be . They 're gentle , " Krause said . " It breaks my heart . . . people are still frightened of them . It 's sad to say that if it looks like a rat , you want to kill it . " 
@@797544 <h> Greenland ice sheet may melt if temperatures increase by just 1.6 degrees Celsius <h> The tipping point for the ice sheet melting is much lower than previous studies indicated , researchers write in Nature . <p> In this July 21 , 2011 photo , an iceberg floats in the sea near Qeqertarsuaq , Disko Island , Greenland . A newly published study in Nature Climate Change says the entire Greenland ice sheet could melt if global warming exceeds 1.6 degrees Celsius . <p> By:Debra BlackStaff Reporter , Published on Mon Mar 12 2012 <p> The Greenland ice sheet may melt completely if there is an increase in global warming by 1.6 degrees Celsius -- a much lower increase in temperatures than previous studies have indicated . <p> A new study published in Nature Climate Change by a team of researchers at the Potsdam Institute for Climate Impact Research and the Universidad Complutense de Madrid used mathematical models and computer simulations of the ice sheet and the regional climate of Greenland to determine the melting point . <p> While it establishes a new tipping point , so to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the researchers said . The melting of the ice sheet would take place over time . <p> " It depends on how high above the threshold we go , " Alexander Robinson , one of the authors and a post doctoral researcher and physicist at both institutions told the Star . <p> " If we had a constant temperature ( increase ) of 2 degrees Celsius it would decline or melt over 50,000 years . " <p> Still the extended period of time that it would take does not take away from the seriousness of the event . And if the temperature increase is from global warming is higher , it will not only rapidly speed up the process but will have profound consequences for the planet . <p> For example if the increase in global warming was 8 degrees Celsius that would mean the whole ice sheet would melt in 2,000 years -- 20 per cent would happen in the first 500 years . <p> And that would mean according to Robinson that you would have 30 centimetres per century of sea level rise . <p> This could have profound @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ if the ice sheet melts it will eventually cause a rise in sea levels worldwide . <p> Previous studies had put the benchmark or tipping point for the Greenland ice sheet to melt at a 3.1 degree Celsius increase in global temperatures . <p> But this new study calls that into question . It also shows the seriousness of the consequences of global warming . <p> " What we 're doing to the planet today has implications for the earth for a long time into the future , " said Robinson . <p> " If we go above this threshold we 're ensuring within a certain period of time there will be seven metres of sea level rise -- that 's the entire volume of water contained inside the Greenland ice sheet . " <p> Currently , the temperature has already risen 0.8 degrees Celsius , Robinson said . " So from today we only need another 0.8 degrees Celsius to reach that threshold " before the ice sheet begins to melt -- albeit slowly . <p> " What it shows is very soon we may take the first step across @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's very hard to decrease increases in temperature . As long as CO2 is in the atmosphere temperatures will stay warm for a long time . <p> " And even if temperatures cool later -- if we 're able to cool the climate -- once the loss of ice has begun and ice has started melting it easily becomes irreversible once you lose a certain amount of ice .
