@@831797 <h> Switch on Your Lights for " Earth Hour " Tonight <p> Tonight , from 8:30 to 9:30 p.m , we 're being told to turn off the lights in celebration of something called " Earth Hour . " And we 're being told to do so by all those enlightened progressive types so as we can collectively take a stand against " AGW " ( Anthropogenic Global Warming - a.k.a. , human-caused climate change . ) <p> Yes , folks , we 're supposedly killing the planet thanks to our cars and our home heating oil and everything else that gives us a comfortable lifestyle . And unless we revert to living in grass huts and forage for grubs , planet earth is deader than disco . <p> But as Public Enemy once crooned : " Do n't believe the hype . " <p> Here are two good reasons to keep your lights blazing come Saturday night when we should be " celebrating " Earth Hour . <p> It is perversely amusing to see thousands of Earth Hour adherents generate illumination during Earth Hour by lighting candles @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Earth Hour to REDUCE carbon emissions ? Where is the logic in eschewing clean-burning electricity - and by the way , 96% of the electricity in the province of Ontario IS clean-generated - in favour of illumination obtained from a power source ( i.e. , open fire ) that generates completely unfiltered CO2 emissions ? <p> Indeed , most of the candles sold in Canada are made from paraffin , a petroleum byproduct . Thus , illuminating one 's house with paraffin candles is equivalent to letting a diesel engine idle . <p> Oh , and how 's this for an inconvenient goof ? Turning off the lights in a skyscraper only to reactivate those lights an hour later actually consumes MORE power than letting those lights remain on for the duration of that hour . Oh sure , it makes for a nifty photo op - but what , pray tell , is the point ? Is Earth Hour simply about feel-good optics rather than tangible solutions ? <p> The second reason to take a pass on Earth Hour : Sponsor Hypocrisy <p> I find it a tad rich to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ doing everything in its power to reduce its environmental footprint . Case in point : Earth Hour Canada sponsor the Toronto Star . To quote the Star verbatim : <p> " We are committed to reporting on and bringing awareness to environmental issues that matter to Canadians . We believe that action on climate change is needed and that Earth Hour is a way to bring attention to this URGENT issue facing us all . " <p> Well , if there 's such enviro-urgency afoot , why does n't the Star choose to reduce its carbon emissions and save millions of trees in the process by no longer publishing a hard copy version of the paper ? After all , the technology exists for the Star to be delivered electronically ... except that might not be so good for business as many readers and advertisers actually still prefer a paper-based product . Stop the presses in the name of Mother Earth ? Not a chance . " Business is business " after all . <p> Bottom line : if you want to experience Earth Hour 24/7 , 365 days a year @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ North Korea . And if you receive permission to touch down in the Hermit Kingdom , feel free to ask your average North Korean how wonderful it is to shiver in the dark and eat grass on a daily basis . <p> In the meantime , let 's all reject the Earth Hour hoax . " Fight back against this insanity on Saturday night by turning ON every light , appliance , and gadget in your house . Celebrate this particular 60 minutes in glorious electrical illumination . And let 's refer to this wonderful celebration for what it really is : Human Achievement Hour . <p> It 's a great time to be alive , for we are living in a golden age . Do n't let a bunch of disingenuous naysayers tell you otherwise . 
@@831916 <h> Snow geese bringing trouble to the tundra <p> Lush , dark green Arctic grasses stand knee high and sway inside a doghouse-sized enclosure fence at La P ? rouse Bay near Churchill . On the outside of the fence , lifeless mud flats stretch beyond the horizon . It 's not an inviting environment for Arctic wildlife . And it probably will never be again . <p> Several small enclosures were put in place in the 1980s by researchers who were already seeing massive degradation of the landscape . They wanted to preserve a plot to compare results down the road . It was n't climate change or pollution they were worried about . It was snow geese . Millions and millions of snow geese . <p> DUCKS UNLIMITED PHOTO <p> A gigantic gaggle of snow geese . <p> Too many birds are cramming into the limited habitat surrounding Hudson Bay , the traditional breeding grounds for the mid-continent population of lesser snow geese . The birds come to the north to breed in the spring , then migrate south through the United States for the winter @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cent over the past three decades . <p> When studies first began at La P ? rouse Bay in 1968 , there were 2,000 pairs of nesting snow geese . By 1990 , there were 22,500 pairs . Fifteen years ago , there were over 40,000 pairs . The news has n't improved . <p> So what has caused this explosive increase ? <p> " Changes in land use , including agricultural changes through increased yields as a result of high yield varieties and fertilizers and establishment of refuges is to blame , " said Frank Baldwin , Game Bird Manager , Wildlife and Ecosystem Protection Branch , Manitoba Conservation and Water Stewardship . " Prior to agriculture and refuges , normal weather variation ( drought ) probably largely governed populations . Stable food supplies during migration and on wintering areas has led to increased survival rates on wintering grounds , and higher productivity , because they arrive on the breeding grounds in better condition . " <p> It 's the aggressive feeding habits of these geese that are converting the once lush and delicate Arctic habitat into ecological wastelands . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ during spring migration , geese ' grub ' the below-ground parts of plants to get the most nutritious bits , which causes near irreversible changes in soil properties , " Baldwin explained . " As the vegetation is removed , the soil dries , becomes more saline and compacted . Ultimately , loss of the seed bank occurs , and other salt-tolerant plants of little nutritive value to geese take over . " <p> With a estimated population of up to 25 million adult snow geese -- three times what the landscape can sustain -- dealing with the situation might seem almost hopeless . But researchers , wildlife managers and conservation groups refuse to give up . <p> " It was determined the most effective way to reduce the population size was to lower adult survival through hunting , " Baldwin said . <p> In the late 1990s , regulatory changes making it a little easier and more attractive to hunt snows on both sides of the border had some success . The highest harvest numbers happened in 1999-2000 , when 1,488,434 geese were taken . Interest from hunters has since @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ like that , the new regulations did little to impact the burgeoning population . <p> " Regardless of specific harvest objectives , increased harvest opportunities did not result in attainment of even the more conservative goals that were originally proposed . Failure to meet harvest objectives likely resulted in part from an under appreciation of population size in the past , leading to an underestimate of the harvest level necessary to meet management goals , " he said . <p> Now , it 's time to take another crack at the problem . <p> " There 's consensus that current efforts to increase harvest should be maintained and expanded where possible . Suggestions for expanding harvest include providing hunters more flexibility to possess , transport and gift snow geese , " he said . <p> Here in Manitoba , a special spring conservation season begins April 1 and ends May 31 . Baldwin said the spring migration has shifted westward in recent years , putting the birds into the extreme western and southwestern reaches of the province . Historically , snows came through the Interlake . <p> " Depending on water @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Delta Marsh and areas west of there , " he added . <p> You 'll need a federal migratory bird permit and a provincial game bird licence to hunt snows . You 'll also need to meet the hunter education requirements that are new this year . Bag limits are a very liberal -- 20 per day and 80 in possession . Keep in mind that you can pass your birds on to someone who wants to give their culinary skills a workout . One fully feathered wing must be attached to each bird along with a signed tag . Check the provincial hunting guide for all the rules and regulations . <p> Shel Zolkewich writes about the outdoors , travel and food when she 's not playing outside , traveling or eating .
